ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e5132	Mr. Vice-Chancellor, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e5147	Of course, the outcome of the two specific pending proceedings cannot be estimated – and not what is the final result of a possible judgment or acquittal. In the event that the ECJ judgment should give rise to a mandatory additional charge of up to three years in pre-service periods, which I have already explained, the Federal Budget threatens an annual additional cost of 5 per cent of staff; this would be about 650 million euros, with which we would then have to calculate. If the federal government also has to pay up to EUR 2.6 billion for the past few years, the total cost of additional costs will be up to EUR 2.6 billion. No more quantifiable additional costs would also arise for the countries and communities, provided that they have comparable schemes in their remuneration rights. In this way, one cannot precisely define it, and we can only speculate. Final sentence: We will keep the damage as low as possible and for the first time ensure that the law does not take place again, but a secure, sustainable repair, so that no further damage arises.

This includes 28 judges plus law firm plus legal advice and everything possible. In addition, there are additional procedural costs of 1.8 million euros. ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e18686	Madam President. Mrs. State Secretary! Ladies and gentlemen, Dear Seers! The asylum and foreign law is being modernised six months ago, becomes more and more inconspicuous, and many practitioners and lawyers say it is hardly possible to implement it. Each Minister of the Interior believes that the screws must be put on even more strongly to prove how strictly he is going against strangers and asylum seekers – so of course this Minister of the Interior. This is more about staging than about substance. I admit, there are content worthy of discussion – so fair I am – there is the idea of better tracking of the escape route and the intensification of home travel; you can discuss it very well. All in all, however, it remains an icon package that we cannot agree to, a symbol package containing measures that do not contain an added value. An example: In the future, asylum seekers are expected to lose cash – as Mr Jenewein has already said – up to EUR 840, so that – I quote the Minister of the Interior – they will contribute to their basic supply costs. This is double absurd, because there is already the Basic Supply Act, which provides that asylum seekers who are able to contest their livelihood from their own resources are not entitled to state benefits and that replacements for the necessary care costs can be prescribed for them. It is absurd that the Minister of the Interior communicates externally, he is now the strict one who agrees that asylum seekers will finally be asked to go to the cash register – the Minister of the Interior of a FPÖ, Mr. Jenewein, who does not want to allow asylum seekers to go to work. That is, those who want to make a contribution are not allowed to do so. Why just? – In order to be able to rush on them, because they are in the basic supply and there cause costs. On the other hand, in the Committee on the Interior, the Minister of the Interior argued that the new cash loss he made is relieved than the current regime. But I have to let him down: Both regulations now apply in parallel! We have a PR Gag: two laws, one More laws, no added value. The Minister of the Interior then made a statement on our questions in the committee, which costs the whole. There is an administrative burden on cash collection of up to €400 000 a year. If we do this now, we recognize that this law provides for the minimum amount of EUR 120 to be left to the person concerned, that the Minister of the Interior should find every year at least EUR 5,000 asylum seekers each year in the luggage to cover the administrative burden at all – we are not talking about a revenue for the state. This is for me a model example of populist envy – there is no cent for the Austrian population. [[Congratulation with NEOS and List Pilz, as well as with representatives of SPÖ.]] Infringement of human rights in this area also does not matter to the Minister of the Interior; we discussed this in the Committee. It goes on: The loss of residence rights or the exclusion from family proceedings should also apply in the future to juvenile offenders. It is about a few cases a year, but it would be an important symbolic sign of human rights. Thank you.

What's this about? – On the one hand, it is about taking action. And those who say we're getting more restrictive now are, of course, right to give. This is exactly what it is all about, because we want to do everything – and we say that quite clearly – in order to avoid a pull effect. We have experienced in 2015 and 2016 that Austria has recorded most migrants in relation to the population after Sweden. We have a great tradition in the reception of refugees, whether it was in the Hungarian crisis, in the Prague spring or in the Yugoslavian war. So Austria cannot be blamed for not always having received quite generous refugees. This was also the case in 2015 and 2016. But there is a border where you simply and simply realize that the population is no longer ready to go there. And to what happened in 2015, you have to say honestly: It is necessary to ensure that such a thing does not happen again and does not repeat itself. [[Abg. Plessl: We did that too!]] Because it is here that there is at all events a contradiction to the Convention on Human Rights, which is in the constitutional rank of us: Whoever thinks there is a constitutional illegality here can sue them before the Constitutional Court. To be honest, that is not an unfair thing, because we also have a duty to defend in other criminal proceedings, and why should the state not provide a legal aid? This is by no means inappropriate, and we know it from other areas. I believe that this law contains very useful measures for the authorities, such as future security and evaluation of data media, such as mobile phones, if there are doubts about the route, the identity and the origin of the person. I believe that it makes sense not to establish a residence in the case of legally condemned offenders in the context of the adoption of a return decision. All of this is measures that are correct and sensible in order not to continue producing a pull effect, as well as an advertising to come to the country, but at the same time to ensure that those who need real asylum within the meaning of the Geneva Convention, therefore, a highly individual right to continue this possibility and not to be blocked by illegal migration, so to speak.

Therefore, at first sight this proposal seems to be very useful, I can only confirm it – but only at first sight, I have to say, because there are considerable differences, and it should not be ignored that are in the legal quality of these institutions and instruments. I should like to remind you that the European Convention on Human Rights is a constitutional one and therefore also the individual right to complain under Article 34 of the ECHR. On the other hand, the International Pact on Civil and Political Rights and the Additional Protocol and the proposed individual complaint are not equipped with this quality of the constitutional rank. All decisions, whether interim measure or judgments of the European Court of Justice, are implemented without delay by Austria. We also know not later than since the decision Mamatkulov versus Turkey of 4. On 1 February 2005, a non-implementation of an interim measure would result in a further infringement of the ECHR and a conviction by the EGMR. My advice to the complainant is therefore quite clear: If you turn to the European Court of Human Rights, because there – and I can say this from my own experience because I worked there for almost two years – is highly professional and detailed. If the EGMR considers that there is no risk of human rights violations – which we are talking about at the interlocutory order – then you can assume that there is no one either. A change of law within the meaning of the NEOS application can be described as desirable, but there are no compelling reasons for this. It was already talking about symbolic policy today, and that is why I would like to say: This too would be symbolic and nothing else, because only because something is not mandatory does not mean that it is not observed. So I do not want to give the impression here that an interim measure is not observed by a human rights committee in Geneva. There is still a case-by-case check. Thank you.

Therefore, as a Parliament, as a legislative force, we should benefit from everything that helps us to prevent these types of culprits. That is why the Federal Government has decided to implement the EU Directive on the use of passenger data, so-called passenger name record data, because the aim of this directive is to combat cross-border activities in the areas of terrorism and serious crime, and to achieve this by evaluating passenger data. This will give the security authorities, the prosecutors, the courts, the intelligence services within their legal powers the opportunity to recognize whether terrorist attacks are threatening or whether organized crime is, so to speak, keeping us involved in air traffic. The implementation takes place in the way – I may briefly explain – that airlines are obliged to transmit these passenger data to the passenger data centre within 24 to 48 hours before the scheduled departure or as soon as passenger-related formalities are completed, and this headquarters is located at the Federal Ministry of the Interior. I have heard from the previous speaker the objection that it is not clear what these data may be used for. This is quite clear because the purpose of the use of the data lies in the prevention, detection and investigation and prosecution of terrorist crimes and serious crime. Many here are lawyers and know where serious crime begins, namely, crimes that are threatened with three years of imprisonment. A use is made It is therefore only in duly justified cases. That's why we're trying to do it. I would like to take a short look at another possibility: This provision applies in principle only to air carriers and flights from or in third countries. However, the EU Directive also allows for the application of this provision by the Minister of State under a regulation. For example, we now have the EU Council Presidency – and this is an event that is associated with significant risks and risks. In this context, it is precisely this directive that allows for the provision of a corresponding possibility in the event of a prognosis by means of a regulation. That's what we're going to do, as far as I know, until 31. December; after that we will not make use of it again. That is why I ask all of you to agree to this draft law.

ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e20180	Madam President. High house! Appreciated spectators on the gallery and at home in front of the televisions! This law is the implementation of the PNR, the so-called passenger name Record Directive, a directive of the European Parliament, the European Union on the prevention and prosecution of terrorist crimes and serious crime. What's this about? – In the future, airlines will have to transmit air passenger data to the passenger data centre before departure or before landing. The law covers only third-country flights, calls flights from the EU to a non-EU country or return. However, it is also right – yes, I am with you – that there is a regulatory authority and that the Federal Minister for the Interior can also provide for an extension, if necessary, and under the same conditions, which also apply to the legal reporting of airlines, namely, when it comes to preventing, persecuting and disclosing terrorist and serious crimes. [[Abg. Lightness: But don't you think the Gold Plating is?]] The data will be registered with the passenger data centre established in the Federal Criminal Office. The task of this passenger data centre is, in addition to the processing of the data, the comparison with relevant police databases, the analysis and also the exchange with Europol and other Member States on occasion. The very clear objective of the directive – I say it again – is the prevention, detection, investigation and prosecution of terrorist crimes and serious crime. We are talking about crimes threatened with more than three years of imprisonment. And the processing is not permitted for any other purpose. Reflexively, such laws or regulations always ask the question of data protection – rightly, ladies and gentlemen, and I would also like to answer this question: The data will be stored for five years in principle, but already after six months a depersonalization has taken place, i.e. no return to the person to whom the data are assigned may be possible. Repersonalisation is limited only if there is a reasonable request from a certain domestic or foreign authority, a security authority, a customs authority or, for example, Europol. [[Abg. Lightenness: What does that mean?]] Depending on the stage of the proceedings, this repersonalization will be ordered by the defendant or by the prosecutor or, if we are already at the stage of the main proceedings, by the judge. In addition, the information must also be passed on to the data protection officer who thus has the control of the legality of the processing of the data. The law also provides – not yet addressed – for an administrative penalty for airlines that do not provide this data in full, not in time or in the required manner. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen, this is necessary if a law is to be effective. If there is no such threat, then we know from the practical experience that very often it is not reported in the prescribed way. Finally, I would like to make one more point: We all report personal data to companies both domestically and abroad on a daily basis. Just think about it: You go Google something on the Internet, you order something at Amazon, you post something on Facebook, where you of course have a registered account, or you book a flight at a foreign airline – even then you announce your data. Consciously or unconsciously, we accept that these data will also be processed. The General Data Protection Regulation has brought a certain sensitivity to the handling of these data, for consumers as well as for businesses. In contrast to commercial use, however, data transmission in this law serves the high objective of preventing, informing and persecuting serious crimes and terrorist crimes. There are very clear rules for the processing of the data, and unfortunately, we must say that in times like this we must be prepared for such cases. I am therefore convinced that access to the data, as it is now provided for in the law, is really justified for this high objective. I would therefore ask you to give your full support to this. Thank you.

The main developments of this Protocol compared to the 2004 Treaty concern cooperation in the following areas: cross-border post-rails, i.e. the implementation of post-rails from a third country as well as the pursuit of a person who is deprived of police control; a mixed strip service with a ten-kilometre spatial limitation for the implementation of mixed strips; and some other things, which will be covered for the first time by this Protocol, such as police transit, cross-border rail and shipping measures, but also support measures to ensure road safety and fight corruption. ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e22508	Mr Tanja Graf. — (DE) Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to speak on behalf of - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e22532	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Dear spectators! First of all, I should like to thank all the members of the committee of all political groups for their good cooperation, because improving the part-time reintegration after a year of practical testing has been a wish of all of us. The reintegration part-time is an absolute success model. By the end of March of this year, more than 1,500 workers used the possibility of gradually returning to their profession after at least six weeks of illness. It was agreed that the normal working time after the illness could be reduced by half and a quarter. Due to the gentle return to the job, overloads and relapses should be avoided and reintegration into the operation easier. Although the salary is reduced by the holding according to the part-time benefit, the loss of money was partly covered by the health insurance funds. For employers, especially in small and medium-sized enterprises, this model does not mean any additional financial burden, but rather an additional support that allows them to keep experienced workers on their premises during the period of lack of personnel. One important point that we are now re-adjusting is the following: So far, immediately after the illness, it was necessary to decide whether to use the reintegration part-time or not. However, practice has shown that workers have often completed their activity too quickly and only later have found that they are not yet ready to work all day long. However, it was unfortunately too late for the application for the reintegration part-time. We want to solve this problem now. In the future, committed workers may also apply for a job within one month after returning to the job, but any personal health and recovery history must be considered individually and differently. We do not want to put anyone in the way of bureaucratic obstacles after serious illness, but we want to support these people as best we can. The flexible application, which is now being created, is in the interests of all parties involved and will contribute even more to the reintegration of people into working life after long-term illness. It is therefore a triple win situation: The workers benefit from job retention and higher cash benefits than in the infirmary, the employers through the maintenance of their employees and our social system through preventive intervention. I am therefore seeking your support for this important and necessary change of law. Thank you.

To achieve this, essential quality standards need to be observed, and we need clinical trials that show a clearly positive benefit-risk analysis. It is a balanced threefold between Senate, Rectorate and University Councils with different tasks. The rectorate as a management body with rector and vice-rectors has certain tasks, and the Unit has several tasks, including monitoring, monitoring, monitoring, monitoring the work of the Senate and the Rectorate. He has the task of participating in the process of choosing the rector and ultimately making the decision – footing on a proposal for a three-way, which comes out of the Senate. To this end, every expert will tell you that this does not make sense only because of compliance guidelines and the principles of balance between these institutions of the top university management, because it does not comply with the principle of reciprocity insofar as it does not confer the right on the university councils or the Council to reject Senate members. Point Report by the Scientific Committee on Amendment No 278/A(E) by Mr Claudia Gamon, Mr MSc(WU), Mr De Clercq, on the accreditation and audit of courses of study at vocational colleges (251 d.b.)
ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e26864	We shall now proceed to item 20 of the agenda. There was no oral reporting. Mr Rosenberger is speaking on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats) and European Democrats.
ParlaMint-AT_2018-07-05-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00036_d8e26892	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Dear ladies and gentlemen! At the last meeting of the Scientific Committee, the debate on the agenda item on the accreditation procedures for FH studies was very consensual. Thank you also for the fact that we have made it possible to formulate a cross-group, uniform amendment. To complete forms, to write reports, to cross questionnaires and to negotiate on-the-spot checks in our professional life, our daily bread, and then to wait more or less for an approval. This is the subject we are dealing with in professional life, and this is also the subject of this accreditation procedure. The accreditation of higher education courses lasts about nine months. Every seven years, an audit of the entire quality management system of the technical universities is required, which lasts for about one to one and a half years. It is carried out by the Agency for Quality Assurance and Accreditation Austria, called AQ Austria, and is currently subject to revision of the procedures and guidelines for this accreditation and for these audits. The relevant opinions of the University of Applied Sciences Conference and the Private University Conference will be taken into account and new directives will be expected this summer. As short as possible, simple and unbureaucratic as possible, but as precise, strict and detailed as necessary: This is why these directives and the accreditation procedures are concerned, on the one hand, with the aim of ensuring that vocational education institutions can work unbureaucratically, simply and well, and on the other hand to ensure that the quality is safeguarded accordingly. These accreditation procedures should not prevent the development of higher education, as envisaged, and it is also envisaged that the universities of higher education, which have really been a success story since 1994, can continue to develop accordingly. What is the feature or what are the criteria of success? – Low drop-out rates, a very short study duration and a close connection with the economy, mostly regional, all despite access restrictions and admission procedures and most moderate study fees. We see that the social mix in the high school system is much higher than at the universities – where all the things I have listed are usually regarded as devils. It has also recently shown concern for the presentation of the high-school development plan, which is in work and is then published. The budget can also be continued: The double budget has been decided that 450 new courses will be put into service in autumn of this year. Also there, the technical universities can continue to work with predictable framework conditions accordingly. The government programme also provides for the quantitative extension of the higher education system. The longer-term target is 60 000 places; at present we are at 50 000. Since we are constantly experiencing that the government programme is being dealt with on a point-by-point basis, the technical universities will also face a good, prosperous future. Thank you.

It has been allocated to the Committee on Health, unanimously with the support of all the political groups, but we must also say that it is too late! Then: The petition was there, and the Committee on Petitions could have assigned it to the Health Committee in good time. At the time, however, the majority of people agreed on the need to: Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report. To say that it is a unanimous decision, is a bit – well, let's say it that way – threatened. The other topic that has occupied me is the postal distribution center in Korneuburg. This sounds very regional, is also very regional. But it is also a perfect example of how we can live parliamentarianism properly if we want people and Parliament, citizens and Parliament to interact, so that a discourse actually arises. However, to do so, we must also bring the topics that concern people in everyday life into the high house. The aim of this postal distribution centre was to focus on environmental concerns, because a logistics centre between two Natu-ra-2000 areas would have been positioned in a fine dust area. That is, one actually had very concrete concerns that damage to humans, animals and the environment could result. There was then the Lower Austrian country election. This citizens' initiative was very loud, and the current head of the Lower Austrian state at the time has envisioned that this logistics centre is not being built. With this argument the opposition parties were also put in question, and there was no agreement on an opinion by the State of Lower Austria. This would have been of great importance, however, because the post still had a one-year option to start construction. The subject is not yet from the table. This brings me to the very central issue of the Committee on Petitions. I've said it more often, but I don't think in this period yet in this clarity: The Committee on Petitions is currently only working as well as the will in the House. The will in the House is currently very limited. It is a matter of increasing the number of issues, trying to reduce the debate to the minimum, but not listening to the citizens. In the last period, this has worked better because there has been a majority opinion. If we want the Committee on Petitions to work in fact, we must make what is actually a good idea of parliamentarians, a right of citizens. We have to think – and this is also a request that I have tabled today to the Rules of Procedure – of the Committee on Petitions from scratch. Especially in times such as those in question – and with this in particular not the black and blue government, but all of Europe – whether democracy and parliamentarianism work, instruments such as the Committee on Petitions are an opportunity to strengthen confidence in politics and to involve people. This does not work with the Committee on Petitions as it is today. What does my request for such a reform involve? It includes, on the one hand, an Internet platform where a debate on individual petitions and parliamentary citizens' initiatives can actually take place. The colleagues of the Freedom Party have found this very well during the last period. We know that a digital debate can contribute significantly to a good image of the public’s mood. For example, such a platform has been in use in the German Bundestag for years and days. A second point, which includes my reform proposal, is that 500 signatures for a parliamentary citizens' initiative do not need to be physically handed over to Parliament, but that a digital signature is sufficient. That's what we're talking 21. If this is sufficiently covered by security measures, it is quite possible. This is also true in several countries, for example in Luxembourg. Another point in which the Committee on Petitions is disadvantaged, from today's point of view, to all the other committees in the House, is the fact that ministries do not have to respond. If a Member of Parliament makes a request to a ministry, it is so that one has to receive an answer within eight weeks. If the Committee on Petitions asks for an opinion, then the Ministry can reply, but does not have to. It has happened more often in the past that it took three or four months, regardless of the party color. The last point – and this is certainly the most important point from my point of view – is that there is the possibility that citizens from a certain number of signatures will also be heard in the Committee on Petitions. Personally, the number of 5 000 signatures floats. Of course, I am ready to negotiate on the scale at any time, but if we want to bring the House and the Committee on Petitions to life seriously and if we want to strengthen the connex between parliamentarians and citizens, then it would be a first, weighty step to say: Those petitions or those citizens' initiatives, which have sufficient support, present their concerns to the Committee within 10 minutes and may also have the possibility of a five-minute debate with the Members. That would be revolutionary for Austria. In the rest of Europe, at least in Western and Central Europe, this is already going on and going on. I would ask you to support this request, if we treat it in the Committee on the Rules of Procedure, with goodwill and with any other proposals for reform. The Petitions Committee was founded in the 1970s and has not seen a serious reform since the early zeros. The world has changed, the people of this world, and the Committee should do the same, as I think. Thank you.
